It’s like… if I eat a normal dinner, or something that feels too rich, like something with butter in it, I’ll be sitting there afterward convinced it’s going to show. Like people will be able to look at me and know I lost control, or that I’m not disciplined anymore. I know that probably doesn’t make sense physically, like one meal can’t literally change everything instantly, but in my head it feels like it can. And I feel really ashamed and anxious, like I’ve failed at something.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat due to your fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I try not to think of it as “restricting,” because that sounds so deliberate, but if I’m being honest, I basically plan my whole day around not really eating much.\n\nLike, before studio I’ll just have coffee, and I’ll tell myself that’s fine because I’m busy and I’ll eat later, but then “later” becomes this whole negotiation in my head. If I’m around other people, I might get something from the café and have a few bites so it doesn’t look weird, but I’m very aware of how much I’m eating and what it is. I’ll pick at it more than actually eat it.\n\nAnd I avoid certain things completely, especially bread or anything oily or buttery. It feels like if I eat that, it’ll undo whatever control I’ve managed to keep that day. I know that probably sounds extreme, but it’s like once I’ve kept things “safe” for most of the day, eating something like that feels like ruining it. So yes… the fear definitely affects how much I let myself eat.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, the number has been going down. That’s what student health keeps focusing on, and it’s basically why they sent me here. They said my weight is too low for them to clear me for the youth workshops, and they wouldn’t really let it go.\n\nI guess I don’t totally experience it the way they do, though. Like, they say it as if it’s this big alarming thing, and I can see on paper that it’s dropped, but in my head it feels more like… I’ve been really focused and busy, and when I get like that, I just sort of keep going and don’t think about it the same way. So yes, I’ve lost weight over the past few months, and apparently enough that they’re concerned, but it’s hard for me to see it as significant in the way everyone else seems to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had your period in the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t track it very carefully. I know that probably sounds irresponsible, but I just don’t really like paying attention to it, and sometimes I’ll sort of assume it’s coming and then not think about it again.\n\nI can’t give you a clear date or anything. I’d have to look back or check my calendar, if I even put it there. I know there have been times where I thought, like, “Oh, it’s been a while,” but then I sort of pushed it out of my mind because I didn’t want to make it into another thing people were going to be worried about.\n\nSo… I don’t know for sure. I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any menstrual periods at all in the past few months?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know for sure. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very helpful answer, especially when you’re asking it directly like that.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had a proper one recently, but then I start second-guessing myself, because sometimes I’ll have, like, cramps or I’ll think maybe it’s about to happen, and then I don’t really follow up in my head. I kind of avoid keeping track because it makes me anxious, and then when someone asks me, I realize I actually don’t have a clear answer.\n\nSo… in the past few months, I’m not sure. I can’t honestly say yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any menstrual periods at all in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a yes-or-no answer, and I’m making it harder than it needs to be. I really don’t have a definite answer.\n\nIf you’re asking specifically the last three months, I can’t clearly remember having a normal period in that time, but I also haven’t been tracking it in a way where I feel confident saying “no, definitely none.” I tend to notice maybe one thing, like feeling like it might start, and then I just kind of avoid thinking about it after that. So I don’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nI guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure. I can’t say that I’ve had one in the past three months.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be fat even if you weighed the same as other people your age and height?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I don’t like saying that because it sounds irrational when you phrase it that way, like if I weighed the same as other people my age and height then I should be able to see myself as the same, but I don’t think that’s how my brain actually works.\n\nLike, when I look at photos from the showcase, I don’t even really see the artwork first, which is embarrassing because that was supposed to be the whole point. I immediately zoom in on my arms and my waist and start checking whether I look small enough. And if I do look small, I feel like… okay, I’m serious, I’m put-together, I’m doing something right. If I don’t, then I just feel wrong, like everyone else can probably see it too.\n\nSo even if the number matched other people’s, I think I’d still be looking for the parts of me that seemed too big. I don’t think I’d trust it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you couldn't stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, because I don’t always know what counts as a “large amount.” Like, compared to what feels like a lot to me, or compared to what would actually be a lot for someone else?\n\nI don’t think I’ve had times where I was eating and genuinely felt like I couldn’t stop in the way you mean, like completely out of control. There have been times where I felt upset afterward or like I’d eaten more than I meant to, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. It’s more like I get really anxious about what I ate, not necessarily that I was eating huge amounts and couldn’t stop myself.\n\nSo… I don’t think so, but I’m not totally sure if I’m interpreting the question the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean, like where I’m eating and it feels like my body is just doing it and I can’t stop.\n\nI can get really upset while eating, or afterward, and feel like I shouldn’t have eaten something, or like I made the wrong choice. And sometimes if I’ve already started eating something that feels “bad” to me, I’ll get kind of panicky and the whole thing feels messy in my head. But that’s different from feeling out of control, I think. It’s more like I’m very aware of it, maybe too aware of it.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I haven’t really felt like I lost control while eating. Or at least I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that perfectly. I don’t think I have a clear “yes” to that. Like, it’s not something I think of as a regular thing I do, or something I have a routine around.\n\nThere have been times after eating where I’ve felt really uncomfortable or panicky, and I’ve wanted the feeling to go away, but I’m not sure that’s the same as what you’re asking. If you mean, like, intentionally making myself throw up after meals as a pattern… I don’t think so. I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nSorry, I know that’s kind of a careful answer. I just don’t want to say yes to something if I’m not sure it fits.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I know what laxatives are, mostly, but I’m not even totally sure I would recognize all the things that count as diuretics unless someone told me. But if you mean taking something specifically so I can lose weight or undo eating or make the number go down, I don’t think that’s something I do.\n\nI’ve definitely thought a lot about weight and food and what different things might do to my body, but with laxatives or water pills specifically… no, I don’t really have an example of using them that way. I’d be kind of scared to, honestly, because I don’t fully understand what they would do. So my answer is no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you thought was excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I think it depends what counts as “excessive,” because my sense of what’s a lot is probably not very objective.\n\nThere are times when I’ll eat something and afterward it feels like, in my head, it was way too much, even if I know another person might look at it and think it was normal or not that much. Like I can get really stuck on the feeling that I overdid it, or that I shouldn’t have had that much, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as actually eating an excessive amount in a short time.\n\nIf you mean like a really large amount of food all at once, where someone else would also say, “That was a lot,” I don’t really know. I don’t think I have a clear example that I can point to. It’s more that the amount can feel excessive to me, even when I’m not sure it really is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that felt excessive to you in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if you phrase it that way, then yes, probably. Not like I’m saying it was objectively some huge amount, because I honestly don’t trust my own judgment about that, but there are definitely times where, in the moment or right after, it feels excessive to me.\n\nIt can be something that maybe sounds normal if I said it out loud, but in my head it feels like, “That was too much, I shouldn’t have done that,” and then I get really preoccupied with it. The “short period of time” part is harder for me to judge, because sometimes it’s more like I’m aware of everything I ate within a certain window and it sort of piles up mentally, even if it wasn’t dramatic or anything.\n\nSo… yes, I’ve had times where it felt excessive to me. I just don’t know if someone else would call it excessive, and that’s the part I get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to say it like that, because it sounds awful, like I’m admitting I think that’s all I am. But if I’m being honest, it does feel like my weight or shape ends up deciding how I feel about myself more than almost anything else.\n\nLike, there was this showcase with photos afterward, and when I looked at them I didn’t really even see the artwork first, which is embarrassing because that was supposed to be the point. I just immediately zoomed in on my arms and my waist, like checking how much space I took up in the picture. And if I looked small enough, then I felt like… okay, I looked serious, or disciplined, or put-together. Like it meant I was doing something right.\n\nAnd if I don’t look the way I think I should, it’s hard for me to separate that from who I am as a person. I know logically someone’s worth shouldn’t depend on that, and I wouldn’t judge someone else that way. But for me, it kind of feels like my body is evidence of whether I’m in control or whether I’m failing. So… yeah, I guess it is one of the biggest factors, even if I hate saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were eating very rapidly during one of these times when you felt like you ate an excessive amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve really paid attention to the speed in that way.\n\nWhen I’m in one of those moments, I’m usually much more focused on the fact that I’m eating at all, or on what it means, or how it’s going to feel afterward. So I don’t know if I’m actually eating very rapidly, like noticeably faster than normal, or if it just feels kind of rushed in my head because I’m anxious and aware of every bite.\n\nThere might be times where I’m not eating slowly, exactly, but I can’t really say, “Yes, I was eating very rapidly.” I don’t have a clear memory of that being the main thing. It’s more the feeling afterward that stands out to me than the pace.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. Like… sometimes I feel very controlled, but not necessarily in a good way. It’s not always “I’m calmly choosing what I want to eat.” It’s more like there are rules running in the background, and I’m trying to follow them, and if I deviate even a little it can feel like everything is slipping.\n\nBut during the times where I feel like I ate too much, I don’t know if I’d say I’m out of control exactly. I don’t have this clear sense of, like, “I literally couldn’t stop myself,” or that my body took over. It’s more confusing than that. I might keep eating while also thinking the whole time that I shouldn’t be, and then I’m very aware of it, almost too aware. So it doesn’t feel mindless, but it also doesn’t feel free.\n\nI guess I feel… trapped by it more than out of control? Like even when I’m making the decision, it doesn’t feel like a normal decision. It feels loaded, and tense, and like whatever I do is going to mean something about me afterward. So I’m not sure if that counts as feeling in control or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you had no menstrual periods at all in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I know that sounds like something I should be able to just say yes or no to, but I don’t track it very consistently, and I get kind of uncomfortable thinking about it too directly.\n\nI don’t have a clear, exact memory of the last few months in that way. Like, I know there have been times where I’ve wondered if it was late or if I was supposed to have gotten it by then, but I haven’t been writing it down or anything. So I don’t want to say “yes, definitely none at all” if I’m not completely sure.\n\nI guess what I can say is that it hasn’t felt regular or predictable to me, but whether it’s been absolutely no periods for three months… I’d have to think about it more or maybe check, if I had any way to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever engaged in self-induced vomiting or other forms of purging after eating?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that fully. I know what you mean by vomiting, obviously, but “purging” feels like a broader word, and I’m not sure exactly what all you’re including in that.\n\nI don’t have a clear thing I can point to and say, yes, I definitely do that after eating. It’s not something I’ve been tracking or even thinking of in those terms. After I eat, I can get really caught up in how I feel about it and what I think I should do next, but I’m not sure if what you’re asking about applies to me.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. If you mean specifically making myself throw up after eating, I don’t have a clear yes to that. But if you mean other things too, I might need you to explain what counts, because I don’t want to answer wrong or make it sound more certain than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t have a clear yes to that. I know that’s probably frustrating because it’s a very specific question, but I’m trying not to answer in a way that sounds more definite than it feels in my head.\n\nLike, when you say “made yourself vomit,” I’m thinking of intentionally doing it after eating, and I don’t have a specific thing I can point to and say, yes, that’s something I’ve done. It’s not something I think of as part of my usual pattern or something I can clearly describe happening.\n\nI feel kind of embarrassed even talking about it, because I keep wondering if I’m misunderstanding what counts or if there are things people mean by that that I’m not thinking of. But if you mean literally causing myself to throw up after eating, I don’t have a clear yes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more that the amount can feel excessive to me, even when I’m not sure it really is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that felt excessive to you in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if you phrase it that way, then yes, probably. Not like I’m saying it was objectively some huge amount, because I honestly don’t trust my own judgment about that, but there are definitely times where, in the moment or right after, it feels excessive to me.\n\nIt can be something that maybe sounds normal if I said it out loud, but in my head it feels like, “That was too much, I shouldn’t have done that,” and then I get really preoccupied with it. The “short period of time” part is harder for me to judge, because sometimes it’s more like I’m aware of everything I ate within a certain window and it sort of piles up mentally, even if it wasn’t dramatic or anything.\n\nSo… yes, I’ve had times where it felt excessive to me. I just don’t know if someone else would call it excessive, and that’s the part I get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important factor in determining your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to say it like that, because it sounds awful, like I’m admitting I think that’s all I am. But if I’m being honest, it does feel like my weight or shape ends up deciding how I feel about myself more than almost anything else.\n\nLike, there was this showcase with photos afterward, and when I looked at them I didn’t really even see the artwork first, which is embarrassing because that was supposed to be the point. I just immediately zoomed in on my arms and my waist, like checking how much space I took up in the picture. And if I looked small enough, then I felt like… okay, I looked serious, or disciplined, or put-together. Like it meant I was doing something right.\n\nAnd if I don’t look the way I think I should, it’s hard for me to separate that from who I am as a person. I know logically someone’s worth shouldn’t depend on that, and I wouldn’t judge someone else that way. But for me, it kind of feels like my body is evidence of whether I’m in control or whether I’m failing. So… yeah, I guess it is one of the biggest factors, even if I hate saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were eating very rapidly during one of these times when you felt like you ate an excessive amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve really paid attention to the speed in that way.\n\nWhen I’m in one of those moments, I’m usually much more focused on the fact that I’m eating at all, or on what it means, or how it’s going to feel afterward. So I don’t know if I’m actually eating very rapidly, like noticeably faster than normal, or if it just feels kind of rushed in my head because I’m anxious and aware of every bite.\n\nThere might be times where I’m not eating slowly, exactly, but I can’t really say, “Yes, I was eating very rapidly.” I don’t have a clear memory of that being the main thing. It’s more the feeling afterward that stands out to me than the pace.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. Like… sometimes I feel very controlled, but not necessarily in a good way. It’s not always “I’m calmly choosing what I want to eat.” It’s more like there are rules running in the background, and I’m trying to follow them, and if I deviate even a little it can feel like everything is slipping.\n\nBut during the times where I feel like I ate too much, I don’t know if I’d say I’m out of control exactly. I don’t have this clear sense of, like, “I literally couldn’t stop myself,” or that my body took over. It’s more confusing than that. I might keep eating while also thinking the whole time that I shouldn’t be, and then I’m very aware of it, almost too aware. So it doesn’t feel mindless, but it also doesn’t feel free.\n\nI guess I feel… trapped by it more than out of control? Like even when I’m making the decision, it doesn’t feel like a normal decision. It feels loaded, and tense, and like whatever I do is going to mean something about me afterward. So I’m not sure if that counts as feeling in control or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you had no menstrual periods at all in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I know that sounds like something I should be able to just say yes or no to, but I don’t track it very consistently, and I get kind of uncomfortable thinking about it too directly.\n\nI don’t have a clear, exact memory of the last few months in that way. Like, I know there have been times where I’ve wondered if it was late or if I was supposed to have gotten it by then, but I haven’t been writing it down or anything. So I don’t want to say “yes, definitely none at all” if I’m not completely sure.\n\nI guess what I can say is that it hasn’t felt regular or predictable to me, but whether it’s been absolutely no periods for three months… I’d have to think about it more or maybe check, if I had any way to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever engaged in self-induced vomiting or other forms of purging after eating?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know how to answer that fully. I know what you mean by vomiting, obviously, but “purging” feels like a broader word, and I’m not sure exactly what all you’re including in that.\n\nI don’t have a clear thing I can point to and say, yes, I definitely do that after eating. It’s not something I’ve been tracking or even thinking of in those terms. After I eat, I can get really caught up in how I feel about it and what I think I should do next, but I’m not sure if what you’re asking about applies to me.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I’m not sure. If you mean specifically making myself throw up after eating, I don’t have a clear yes to that. But if you mean other things too, I might need you to explain what counts, because I don’t want to answer wrong or make it sound more certain than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t have a clear yes to that. I know that’s probably frustrating because it’s a very specific question, but I’m trying not to answer in a way that sounds more definite than it feels in my head.\n\nLike, when you say “made yourself vomit,” I’m thinking of intentionally doing it after eating, and I don’t have a specific thing I can point to and say, yes, that’s something I’ve done. It’s not something I think of as part of my usual pattern or something I can clearly describe happening.\n\nI feel kind of embarrassed even talking about it, because I keep wondering if I’m misunderstanding what counts or if there are things people mean by that that I’m not thinking of. But if you mean literally causing myself to throw up after eating, I don’t have a clear yes."
  }
